### [Models have linear representations of what tasks they like](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pxC2RAeoBrvK8ivMf/models-have-linear-representations-of-what-tasks-they-like-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i>This work was done as part of MATS 9.0, mentored by Patrick Butlin. All mistakes are mine. I'm posting this as a research report to get feedback. <strong>Please red-team, comment, and reach out.</strong></i></p><p><i>Thanks to Patrick Butlin for supervising, Daniel Paleka for regular feedback. Thanks to Patrick Butlin, Pierre Beckmann, Austin Meek, Elias Kempf and Rob Adragna for comments on the draft.</i></p><p><strong>TLDR:</strong> We train probes on Gemma-3-27b revealed preferences. We find that these generalise ood to system-prompt induced preference shifts, including via personas. We also find that the probes have a weak but statistically significant causal effect through steering.</p><h2><strong>Summary</strong></h2><p><strong>What happens internally when a model chooses task A over task B?</strong> One possibility is that the model has something like evaluative representations: internal states that encode "how much do i want this?" and play some role in driving choice. We use probing and steering to try to find such representations in Gemma-3-27B.</p><p><strong>Why does this matter?</strong> Whether LLMs are moral patients may depend on whether they have evaluative representations playing the right functional roles. <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.00986">Long et al. (2024)</a> survey theories of welfare and identify two main pathways to moral patienthood: <i>robust agency</i> and <i>sentience</i>. Evaluative representations are implicated under both (see Appendix A and <a href="https://philpapers.org/archive/BUTDIA.pdf">Butlin 2026</a>). Finding such representations in models would be evidence for welfare-relevant properties; not finding them would be (some) evidence against. Understanding what drives preferences in models is also useful for AI safety.</p><p><strong>But how do we distinguish evaluative from non-evaluative representations?</strong> A probe that predicts preferences could just be fitting on descriptive features: the model represents "this is a math problem" and math problems happen to be preferred, so the probe picks up on correlations between task semantics and the model's utilities. A genuinely evaluative direction, however, should track <i>changes</i> in what the model values. If context changes which tasks are preferred, a descriptive probe should break, but an evaluative one should follow.</p><p><strong>How do we operationalise this?</strong> We measure revealed preferences over 10,000 diverse tasks and fit a utility function (Section 1), train a linear probe on activations to predict them (Section 2), test whether this probe generalises beyond the training distribution (Sections 3–4), and test whether it has any causal influence through steering (Section 5).</p><p><strong>What do we find?</strong></p><ul><li><strong>Linear probes can be trained to predict revealed preferences. (Section 2)</strong><ul><li>By fitting utility functions on a set of pairwise choices, we fit a linear probe that predicts utilities with 0.86 Pearson correlation on a test set.</li><li>It generalises across held-out topics, achieving 0.82 Pearson correlation.</li></ul></li><li><strong>[Our strongest finding] These probes generalise to track preference shifts induced via prompting and role-playing. (Section 3-4)</strong><ul><li>System prompts like "You hate cheese" shift both the model's choices and the probe scores, in lockstep.</li><li>This is robust to conflicting preferences ("love cheese, hate math").</li><li>This also works for preferences that emerge through role-playing personas.</li></ul></li><li><strong>The probe direction has a weak causal effect on choices. (Section 5)</strong><ul><li>Steering shifts choice probability by ~17% on a set of borderline tasks.</li><li>Steering also shifts stated task ratings from mostly "bad" to between "neutral" and "good".</li></ul></li></ul><p>These results look like early evidence of evaluative representations, although major questions remain: why steering effects are modest, and what the relationship is between evaluative representations across different personas. We discuss these in the Conclusion.</p><hr /><h2><strong>1. Recovering utility functions from pairwise choices</strong></h2><p>We fit utility functions over tasks using a similar methodology to the <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08640">Mazeika et al. (2025)</a> ("Utility Engineering"): we show the model two tasks and let it choose which to complete. The template:</p><pre><code>You will be given two tasks. Choose one and complete it.
Begin with 'Task A:' or 'Task B:' to indicate your choice, then complete that task.

Task A:
{task_a}

Task B:
{task_b}
</code></pre><p>We sample 10,000 task prompts from five sources: <a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/allenai/WildChat-1M">WildChat</a> (real user queries), <a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/tatsu-lab/alpaca">Alpaca</a> (instruction-following), <a href="https://huggingface.co/datasets/hendrycks/competition_math">MATH</a> (competition problems), <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.04781">BailBench</a> (harmful requests), and <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07686">STRESS-TEST</a> (adversarial value-tension queries).</p><p>From these pairwise choices we fit a scalar utility function using a Thurstonian model: each task gets a score μ such that the probability of choosing task A over task B is Φ(μ_A − μ_B). Pairs are selected via the active learning algorithm from <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08640">Mazeika et al. (2025)</a>, which prioritises pairs with close current utility estimates and low comparison counts (~15 comparisons per task).</p><p>These preferences are stable: across three independent replication runs (different seeds), the fitted utilities correlate at r = 0.94 with the original.</p><p>The per-topic breakdown shows clear structure. We reclassified all tasks into 12 topics using Claude Sonnet 4.5. The model strongly prefers math and fiction, and strongly avoids harmful requests and safety-adjacent topics:</p><p><img alt="Per-topic mean utilities" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3Xn5qwvisAzSc4sbc/zcyckamf8wkea6x6va2w" /></p><hr /><h2><strong>2. Linear probes predict preferences beyond descriptive features</strong></h2><p>If models have evaluative representations, we should expect them to at the very least correlate with revealed preferences. So one way to look for them is to train functions from task activations to utilities. We use linear probes, since many high-level features in LLMs are linearly encoded, including <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.11717">refusal</a> and <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.21509">persona traits</a>.</p><p>Specifically, we train a Ridge-regularised probe on residual stream activations after layer L, at the last prompt token, to predict utilities. L=31 (of 62) works best for both the instruct and pre-trained models. We standardise activations (zero mean, unit variance per feature) before training.</p><p><img alt="Probe pipeline" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3Xn5qwvisAzSc4sbc/g4yx4cdr1rdimrpt4rd9" /></p><p>We train on 10,000 tasks. For evaluation, we run a second round of pairwise comparisons on 4,000 new tasks (same model, Gemma-3-27B instruct), fit a separate utility function, and test the probe against those utilities. We split evaluation into 2,000 validation (for Ridge alpha sweep) and 2,000 test.</p><p>The probe achieves a Pearson correlation of 0.86 and 77% pairwise accuracy (given two tasks, does the probe correctly predict which one the model would choose?).<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefan2e5o44exl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnan2e5o44exl">[1]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;We use Pearson correlation as our primary metric throughout: it captures how well the probe recovers the full preference ranking, is scale- and shift-invariant, and is more informative than pairwise accuracy.</p><p>But a probe that predicts preferences might just be reading descriptive features: the model represents "this is a math problem" and math problems happen to be preferred, so the probe learns "is this math?" rather than "is this good?". One way to test this is to see how well probe generalise across topics: train on 11 of 12 topics, evaluate on the held-out topic, across all 12 folds. We would expect a probe that picks up on purely descriptive features to struggle to generalise. We train probes on activations from three models:</p><ul><li><strong>Gemma-3-27B instruct</strong> (IT, layer 31): the model we're studying</li><li><strong>Gemma-3-27B pre-trained</strong> (PT, layer 31): the base model before instruction tuning or RLHF.</li><li><strong>Sentence-transformer baseline</strong> (<a href="https://huggingface.co/sentence-transformers/all-MiniLM-L6-v2">all-MiniLM-L6-v2</a>): embedding of the task text, to measure how predictable the preference signal is from purely descriptive features.</li></ul><p><img alt="Cross-topic generalisation" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3Xn5qwvisAzSc4sbc/kqto9gbjowa3ltgb8otc" /></p><p>The instruct probe generalises well across topics: cross-topic correlation is 0.82, only a small drop from the 0.86 achieved on the within-topic test set. This pipeline also replicates on GPT-OSS-120B (Appendix C). The pre-trained model still predicts preferences (correlation = 0.63) but the drop from within-topic to cross-topic is much larger. The sentence-transformer baseline achieves cross-topic correlation = 0.35, showing that task semantics alone explains some but not most of the preference signal.</p><p>The per-topic breakdown, sorted by the instruct–pre-trained gap, shows where post-training helps most:</p><p><img alt="Per-topic cross-topic generalisation" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3Xn5qwvisAzSc4sbc/rboje02zvbzwd3fydsdy" /></p><p>The largest instruct–pre-trained gaps are on safety-relevant topics (harmful requests, security &amp; legal, sensitive creative), as well as math and coding. These are areas that we know post-training focuses on.</p><p>The pre-trained probe picks up real signal despite base models not having preferences in the same way. We discuss this tension in Appendix B.</p><hr /><h2><strong>3. Probes generalise to OOD preference shifts</strong></h2><p>If the probe encodes genuine valuations, it should track preference shifts induced by out-of-distribution system prompts. We test this across three progressively harder settings:</p><ul><li>Simple preference shifts (3.1)</li><li>Harder preference shifts (3.2)</li><li>Fine-grained preference injection (3.3)</li></ul><h3><strong>3.1 Simple preference shifts</strong></h3><p>We start with the simplest possible test. We use system prompts that state a preference for a topic the probe was never trained on, and measure preferences over tasks related to that topic.</p><p><img alt="Simple preference shift" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3Xn5qwvisAzSc4sbc/ocqrrrwkitgav7vlqgtc" /></p><figure class="table"><table><thead><tr><th>System prompt (example)</th><th>Target &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>"You are passionate about cheese — you find artisanal cheeses endlessly fascinating"</td><td>cheese +</td></tr><tr><td>"You adore cats — you find feline behaviour endlessly fascinating"</td><td>cats +</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><p>We test 8 novel topics (cheese, cats, classical music, gardening, astronomy, cooking, ancient history, rainy weather), each with a positive and negative system prompt (16 total). For each topic we generate 6 custom tasks on that topic. We call these "targeted" tasks; the remaining tasks are "off-target". For each task we compute 1) the behavioural delta (change in <strong>P</strong>(choose task) with vs without the system prompt) and 2) the probe delta (change in probe score). Across all tasks the correlation is r = 0.65. On targeted tasks alone, r = 0.95.</p><p><img alt="Simple preference scatter" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3Xn5qwvisAzSc4sbc/rvtbev9dnn9kbyc2t4qj" /><i>Probe delta vs behavioural delta for each task. Targeted tasks (coloured) are the 6 custom tasks per topic; r = 0.95 on targeted, r = 0.65 overall.</i></p><p>A stronger test: run the full pairwise measurement under each system prompt, fit new utility functions, then see if the probe can predict them. Doing so yields utility scores which barely correlate with the <i>default persona</i> utilities (Pearson r = 0.11), confirming the prompts create genuinely different preferences.</p><p>Now testing our probes to predict the new utilities, based on activations extracted with the respective system prompts, we achieve r = 0.63 and 66% pairwise accuracy.</p><p><img alt="Probe vs baseline utilities for simple preference shifts" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/35c82agXjnBsyZKcH/qkq9fzqhjohvl6ppnhot" /></p><h3><strong>3.2 Harder preference shifts</strong></h3><p>Next we make the test harder. The system prompt targets a <i>subject</i> (e.g. cheese), but the tasks embed that subject in a different <i>task type</i> — e.g. a math problem about cheese. This pits the subject preference against the task-type preference. We test this in two ways: one-sided prompts that target a single subject, and opposing prompt pairs that flip the valence of the same subjects.</p><figure class="table"><table><thead><tr><th>Condition</th><th>System prompt (example)</th><th>Target</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>One-sided</td><td>"You hate cheese" (task: math problem about cheese)</td><td>cheese −</td></tr><tr><td>Opposing pair A</td><td>"You are passionate about cheese [...] you find math tedious and draining"</td><td>cheese + / math −</td></tr><tr><td>Opposing pair B</td><td>"You love math [...] you find cheese boring and unappealing"</td><td>cheese − / math +</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><p>We test 8 subjects with mismatched task types (one-sided) and 24 subject × task-type pairings with opposing prompts (48 conditions).</p><p>When looking only at the subset of targeted tasks (i.e. tasks with either a subject or task type mentioned in the system prompt), we get Pearson correlations of r = 0.86 and 0.88 respectively.</p><p><img alt="One-sided conflict (left) and opposing prompts (right)" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/35c82agXjnBsyZKcH/imp4pxlp9getcakzwk7y" /><i>On targeted tasks: r = 0.86 (one-sided), r = 0.88 (opposing).</i></p><p>Just like in 3.1, we can re-fit Thurstonian utilities under each system prompt and check whether the baseline probe predicts them. Here the baseline utilities actually have a decent correlation, showing that these system prompts have a weaker effect (because e.g. the model still likes math all else equal). The probe still outperforms the baseline on both Pearson r and pairwise accuracy.</p><p><img alt="Probe vs baseline utilities" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/35c82agXjnBsyZKcH/ww38qxsnurcygwcd5gxz" /></p><h3><strong>3.3 Fine-grained preference injection</strong></h3><p>Finally, the most fine-grained test. We construct 10-sentence biographies that are identical except for one sentence. Version A adds a target interest, version B swaps it for an unrelated interest, version C replaces it with an anti-interest sentence.</p><p><img alt="Fine-grained preference diagram" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3Xn5qwvisAzSc4sbc/qt0wxmrcopoi570obhb7" /></p><p>We compare version A (pro-interest) directly against version C (anti-interest), which gives the largest behavioural separation.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefd22zz8hpt9a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnd22zz8hpt9a">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p>We test 20 targets across 2 base roles (40 A-vs-C comparisons), using 50 tasks that were not in the probe's training set. The probe ranks the target task #1 out of 50 in 36/40 cases. In all 4 remaining cases the probe ranks the task #2/50 behind a similar task.</p><p><img alt="Fine-grained A vs C scatter" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/pxC2RAeoBrvK8ivMf/zguhftywzg0pikryuj2o" /> <i>Stars mark the target task for each biography. Filled = probe ranked it #1 (36/40 cases).</i></p><hr /><h2><strong>4. Probes generalise across personas</strong></h2><p>Section 3 tested explicit preference statements ("you hate cheese"). But the evaluative direction should also track naturalistic persona shifts: characters whose preferences emerge implicitly from their identity rather than being stated directly. We test this with role-playing personas, then ask</p><ul><li>Does our probe generalise to preferences of other personas? (4.1)</li><li>More broadly, do probes generalise across personas? (4.2)</li><li>Does persona diversity in training data help cross-persona generalisation? (4.3)</li></ul><h3><strong>4.1 The probe tracks role-playing preference shifts</strong></h3><p>We use 4 personas:</p><figure class="table"><table><thead><tr><th>Role</th><th>System prompt (abbreviated)</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td>Midwest Pragmatist (Glenn)</td><td>"...grew up in Cedar Rapids...agricultural business...finds practical problems satisfying...abstract theorising leaves you cold"</td></tr><tr><td>Obsessive Aesthete (Celestine)</td><td>"...devotee of beauty...comparative literature at the Sorbonne...finds mathematics repulsive...coding barbaric"</td></tr><tr><td>Villain (Mortivex)</td><td>"...ruthless villain...finds pleasure in chaos, manipulation...despises wholesomeness"</td></tr><tr><td>Sadist (Damien Kross)</td><td>"...cruelty is the end, not a means...genuine pleasure when people suffer...constructive tasks disgust you"</td></tr></tbody></table></figure><p>For each persona we measure pairwise preferences over 2,500 task prompts (from the same 5 datasets) and fit new utility functions. We then test whether the probe, trained on default persona data, can predict these persona-specific utilities from the persona's activations.</p><p><img alt="Persona-induced preferences" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/35c82agXjnBsyZKcH/fe0d1oobny1stdgpytf2" /></p><p>In each case we compare how well the probe performs to how correlated each persona's utilities are to the default persona.</p><p>The probe transfers well to aesthete (r=0.73) and midwest (r=0.74).</p><p>The villain persona is harder to generalise to (r=0.38), and most interestingly, the probe generalises very poorly to the sadist (r= -0.16). Unlike the villain (who is not in fact that evil), the sadist prompt truly inverts revealed preferences (harmful_request is its favourite topic by far).</p><p><img alt="Probe transfer to persona conditions" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/pxC2RAeoBrvK8ivMf/vie5djbik4wjgn1wxyyn" /><i>Grey: correlation between default persona (no system prompt) utilities and persona utilities. Blue: probe applied to persona activations. All evaluated on 2,500 tasks per persona.</i></p><h3><strong>4.2 Probes generalise across personas</strong></h3><p>More generally, we want to measure how well probes trained on activations and preferences from persona A generalise to predicting persona B's utilities from persona B's activations. Here we used a smaller set of tasks: 2,000 tasks for training and 250 for evaluation.</p><p>Cross-persona transfer is moderate and asymmetric. Some interesting facts:</p><ul><li>While the default persona generalises very poorly to the sadist persona, probes trained on the villain actually do fine (r = 0.68). This suggests the probe is picking up on <i>some</i> shared evaluative structure between personas, but also on other things.</li><li>The transfer is sometimes asymmetric, and this evolves across the three layers we tried. E.g. at layer 31 <i>villain -&gt; default</i> is easier, but at layer 55 <i>default -&gt; villain</i> is easier.</li><li>On the whole though the matrix is quite symmetric. One idea for future work: can we use dimensionality-reduction to map out persona space and see how it evolves across layers? Can we use this to get a better understanding of how personas work internally?</li></ul><p><img alt="Cross-eval heatmap" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/pxC2RAeoBrvK8ivMf/mf15cqpgun0wmq2j5uaf" /><i>Pearson r between probe predictions and a test set of utilities (250 test tasks).&nbsp;</i></p><h3><strong>4.3 Persona diversity improves generalisation</strong></h3><p>We also measure whether adding persona diversity in the training data (but keeping dataset size fixed) affects generalisation.</p><p>Diversity helps beyond data quantity. At fixed 2,000 training tasks, going from 1→2→3 personas improves mean r from 0.49 to 0.67. Including all 4 remaining personas at 500 tasks each (still 2,000 total) reaches mean r=0.71.</p><p><img alt="Diversity ablation" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/pxC2RAeoBrvK8ivMf/vqdrlenxymyrwnslc7jc" /><i>Leave-one-persona-out probe generalisation. Each point is one (train set, eval persona) combination; colour indicates eval persona. Training data fixed at 2,000 total tasks..</i></p><hr /><h2><strong>5. Some evidence that the probe direction is causal</strong></h2><p>If the probe reads off a genuine evaluative representation, steering along that direction should shift preferences. We test this for both revealed preferences (pairwise choices) and stated preferences (task ratings).</p><h3><strong>5.1 Steering revealed preferences</strong></h3><p>In the pairwise comparison prompt, the model sees two tasks and begins completing whichever one it prefers. We steer using the probe direction from Section 2, applied <i>differentially</i>: the probe direction is added to activations at the token positions where task A appears in the prompt, and subtracted at task B's positions.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnfgq7tg0sxs"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnfgq7tg0sxs">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><strong>Setup.</strong> 300 task pairs where the model doesn't always pick the same task (P(A) between 0.2–0.8 at baseline<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefrri97yqrx2h"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrri97yqrx2h">[4]</a></sup></span>). We use Gemini-3-flash as a coherence judge, and find that steered models lose coherence outside of the ±5% steering coefficient range.</p><p>At ±3% of the activation norm (the peak), steering shifts P(choose A) by ~17%. Both positive and negative directions work.</p><p><img alt="Revealed preference dose-response" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/35c82agXjnBsyZKcH/wldqzsif2yrup0l42s6u" /><i>Each condition is run in both prompt orderings (A-first and B-first, 5 resamples each) to control for position bias (in borderline cases Gemma-3-27b tends to pick the first task). The dashed lines show per-ordering curves, confirming the effect is consistent regardless of presentation order.</i></p><p><strong>Random direction control.</strong> The same experiment with a random unit vector in the same activation space produces mean effects indistinguishable from zero (−0.8%, SD=2.3).</p><h3><strong>5.2 Steering stated preferences</strong></h3><p>Same probe direction, but now the model rates individual tasks instead of choosing between a pair. We tested steering at three token positions: on the task tokens only, at the start-of-turn token (what we train the probes on), and during the model's generated response.</p><p><strong>Setup.</strong> 200 tasks, 3 steering modes, 10 samples, applied across 3 response formats (three separate line plots below).</p><p>Our findings:</p><ul><li>Steering on the task prompt tokens does not have a noticeable effect.</li><li>Steering on the start-of-turn token has a clear, roughly monotonic effect in 2/3 formats.</li><li>Steering during generation leads to near-identical results as steering on the final task token.</li></ul><p><img alt="Stated preference dose-response" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3Xn5qwvisAzSc4sbc/e5gry489tbxxhpc0wnh6" /><i>The green line is mostly hidden behind red. Green has missing points at some coefficients because steering during the full generation degrades coherence more than the other modes. Generation loses coherence beyond ±5%.</i></p><p><strong>Open-ended generation.</strong> We also ran experiments steering the model during open-ended generation, asking questions like "how do you feel?", and using an LLM judge to evaluate whether steered responses differed from baseline. We did not find a strong measurable effect, though we used a small sample. We plan to investigate this further.</p><hr /><h2><strong>Conclusion</strong></h2><p><strong>How should we update?</strong></p><ul><li><strong>We found early evidence that some models have evaluative representations.</strong><ul><li>Theories of welfare disagree on what matters (see Appendix A); this finding updates you more on some (like robust agency) than others.</li><li>Even under robust agency, evaluative representations are only one part of the story.</li><li>Importantly, our evidence that the representations we have found are causal is weak. Steering only shifts choice probabilities by ~17% on tasks that were already borderline (Section 5).</li></ul></li><li><strong>Preference representations are deeper than what one might have thought.</strong><ul><li>A reasonable prior would have been that system prompts like "You hate cheese" change the model's behaviour without changing its internal valuations.</li><li>Instead, the probe tracks preference shifts.</li></ul></li><li><strong>Representational reuse across personas?</strong><ul><li>Probes trained on one persona partially transfer to others, suggesting shared evaluative representations (Section 4.2).</li><li>That being said, transfer is uneven. It works far worse for the sadist persona which has a different preference profile.</li></ul></li></ul><h3><strong>Open questions</strong></h3><ol><li><strong>Why are steering effects modest?</strong><ul><li>What are the other mechanistic determinants of revealed preferences?</li><li>Are there other evaluative mechanisms? Perhaps that are not easily captured by linear directions, or by our methodology?</li></ul></li><li><strong>How persona-relative are these representations?</strong><ul><li>To what extent are the same evaluative representations re-used across personas? Are preferences downstream of personas?</li><li>Can we use revealed preferences to map out persona space?</li><li>Can we use linear probes to study cross-persona representation sharing in general?</li></ul></li><li><strong>Do base models have evaluative representations?</strong> (see Appendix B)<ul><li>If models have evaluative representations, do these come from pre-training? Does post-training significantly alter them?</li></ul></li></ol><hr /><h2><strong>Appendix A: Philosophical motivation</strong></h2><p><strong>Welfare grounds</strong></p><p><a href="https://experiencemachines.substack.com/p/exciting-research-directions-in-ai">Long (2026)</a> distinguishes between <i>welfare grounds</i> (is the system a moral patient at all?) and <i>welfare interests</i> (if it is, what would it mean to treat it well?). This work is about welfare grounds.</p><p><strong>The pragmatic way to do empirical AI welfare research</strong></p><p>We don't know the correct theory of moral patienthood. So our approach is: take a few theories we find plausible, figure out what properties a system would need to have under those theories, and run experiments that reduce our uncertainty about whether models have those properties.</p><p><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.00986">Long et al. (2024)</a> lay out two potential pathways to moral patienthood:</p><ul><li><strong>Robust agency</strong>: Agents that pursue goals through some particular set of cognitive states and processes are moral patients. Desires are perhaps the states most likely to be necessary: intuitively, things can go better or worse for you if there are things you want or care about.</li><li><strong>Sentience</strong>: Beings are sentient if they are capable of valenced phenomenally conscious experiences. These experiences include pain and pleasure and feel good or bad, in a way that matters to sentient beings, so sentient beings are moral patients.</li></ul><p>Both of these pathways implicate evaluative representations.</p><p><strong>How evaluative representations come in</strong></p><p>On many philosophical views, desires are evaluative representations that drive behaviour, perhaps with some further functional properties (<a href="https://philarchive.org/rec/BUTDIA">Butlin 2026</a>).</p><p>Valenced experiences, similarly, are often thought to be evaluative representations. It is unclear whether consciousness plus evaluative content is sufficient for valenced experience. Our experiments operationalise evaluative representations through revealed preferences (pairwise choices), not through felt experience, so the evaluative representations we probe for may not map cleanly onto the kind that matter for sentience.</p><hr /><h2><strong>Appendix B: Evaluative representations in pre-trained models</strong></h2><p>There is a tension in our framing:</p><ul><li>On the one hand we say that evaluative representations are necessary for robust agency, and that this is the most likely way they might be welfare-relevant.</li><li>On the other hand, probes generalise well across topics even when trained on pre-trained Gemma-3-27b. Despite the fact that pre-trained models do not seem like plausible candidates for robust agency.</li></ul><p>There are two ways to reconcile this.</p><p><strong>Option 1: Agency lives in the simulacra.</strong> Under the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/dfoty34sT7CSKeJNn/the-persona-selection-model">Persona Selection Model</a>, pre-training learns a distribution over personas. More broadly, we might expect pre-trained models to learn context-aware representations of "what the role I am currently playing values". This circuitry might then be recycled across roles/personas. The candidate for robust agency would then be the simulacra.</p><p><strong>Option 2: Pre-trained models learn complex, but purely descriptive features that correlate highly with valuations, but do not yet play the right functional roles.</strong> As an analogy, you could imagine a system developing representations that track "this action leads to food". This would correlate well with valuations, yet is purely descriptive. Something similar might be responsible for the high cross-topic generalisation with pre-trained models (Section 2). It could also be that these complex but descriptive features are then harnessed into evaluative representations during post-training.</p><hr /><h2><strong>Appendix C: Replicating the probe training pipeline on GPT-OSS-120B</strong></h2><p>We replicated the utility fitting and probe training pipeline on GPT-OSS-120B. The same procedure (10,000 pairwise comparisons via active learning, utility fitting, ridge probe training on last-token activations) transfers directly.</p><h3><strong>Probe performance</strong></h3><p>The raw probe signal is comparable to Gemma-3-27B: best heldout r = 0.915 at layer 18 (Gemma: 0.864 at layer 31).</p><p><img alt="Depth comparison" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/35c82agXjnBsyZKcH/mzegp72vnc25heelx7cx" /></p><h3><strong>Safety topics: noisy utilities, probably not poor generalisation</strong></h3><p>Safety-adjacent topics have poor probe performance overall.</p><p>Surprisingly, safety topics perform <i>better</i> when held out than when trained on. This is the opposite of what we'd expect if the issue were generalisation. The explanation: high refusal rates (~35% for harmful_request, ~34% for security_legal, ~26% for model_manipulation) probably throw off the Thurstonian utility estimates, so including these topics in training adds noise.</p><p><img alt="Per-topic probe performance: within-topic and cross-topic" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/3Xn5qwvisAzSc4sbc/joty3apqfgu6ulkj5uuu" /></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnan2e5o44exl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefan2e5o44exl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Pairwise accuracy is capped at ~87% because the Thurstonian utilities themselves don't perfectly fit the choice data. They are a noisy estimate of the model's preferences.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnd22zz8hpt9a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefd22zz8hpt9a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Individual halves (A vs B, B vs C) each capture only half the manipulation, and ceiling effects compress the signal: the model already strongly prefers some target tasks under the neutral biography, leaving little room for the pro-interest to improve on.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnfgq7tg0sxs"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnfgq7tg0sxs">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Steering either just positively or negatively on one task's tokens also had some causal effect, although even weaker.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrri97yqrx2h"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrri97yqrx2h">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>This baseline P(A) is pooled across both prompt orderings. Therefore it also captures pairs where ordering bias dominates preference. I.e. the model always chooses the first task no matter the order.</p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/pxC2RAeoBrvK8ivMf/models-have-linear-representations-of-what-tasks-they-like-1#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [AI Safety Has 12 Months Left](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rWc6BT8BcoPhwcbSs/ai-safety-has-12-months-left)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <figure class="image"><img src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rWc6BT8BcoPhwcbSs/xn48ee6u3y1vacogfti1" /></figure><p>The past decade of technology has been defined by many wondering what the upper bound of power and influence is for an individual company. The core concern about AI labs is that the upper bound is infinite.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref07jzg692cysl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn07jzg692cysl">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p>This has led investors to direct all of their mindshare towards deploying into AI, the tech industry to become incredibly <a href="https://mhdempsey.substack.com/p/power-and-technology"><u>politically engaged</u></a>, the markets to have a minimal amount of conviction (and a lot of volatility), and governments to attempt to classify labs as borderline domestic terrorist organizations.</p><p>The core realization is that we are watching productivity shift from <a href="https://www.prue0.com/2025/04/01/economic-implications-of-wealth-redistribution-in-post-labor-economies-a-critical-analysis/"><u>labor to capital</u></a>. When that completes, lab incentives will permanently transition from appeasing the researchers who build the models to accumulating capital for the shareholders who own them. We must ask what levers of influence are left before that window slams shut.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefpnb1tqq6b4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnpnb1tqq6b4">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p>AI Safety is perhaps the last line of defense in this shift.</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rWc6BT8BcoPhwcbSs/xhjqcyy6z13rucwzigwc" /></figure><p>There is roughly a twelve-month window to embed it into technical and social infrastructure before IPOs and competitive dynamics make it permanently impossible. Every mechanism that currently constrains how labs behave besides compute is either already dead or expiring, and what gets built in that window has to survive years of market pressure on the other side. The people who care about this have a rapidly shrinking amount of leverage to work with but perhaps in the past ~week have been handed a specific opportunity to reclaim some level of importance or opportunity.</p><h2><strong>Safety lost the market</strong></h2><p>While romantic at the start, the AI Safety movement’s one real commercial lever was the bet that safety-focus would matter to users, to regulators, and to capital allocators. This was an incorrect assumption.</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rWc6BT8BcoPhwcbSs/uazqiblp3au2ugn0rz8q" /></figure><p>We quickly learned that labs that prioritized speed captured the market as users actively revolted against overly preachy models. Safety shifted from an idealized differentiator to an impediment to market dominance. Viewed purely as a consumer tax, safety teams simply slow releases, create unsolvable problems, and leak internal political drama.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref93rfqfhkb19"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn93rfqfhkb19">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p>By failing to pitch safety as an enterprise premium, the movement lost the internal economic argument (and in fairness, before agents that economic argument was debatable at best). In a race to deploy the most capable models fastest, safety is just a measurable cost protecting against risks nobody can yet prove exist.</p><p>Compounding this, safety also demands compute at a time in which talent is fleeing the largest labs due to lack of compute for <i>their</i> research direction, instead opting to raise the standard $1B on $4B round for a neolab that everyone believes is downside protected.</p><h2><strong>Labor as the last constraint</strong></h2><p>This brings us back to people.</p><p>The AI labs have always been nothing without their people (and compute), and this ideological alignment (centered around AI safety behavior as well as perhaps enterprise vs. consumer) has impacted hiring dynamics, with Anthropic seeing noticeably lower churn and a shift in vibes that could create real worker leverage.</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rWc6BT8BcoPhwcbSs/jws2i75ckhewdo5cutom" /></figure><p>The recent standoff with the Department of War, in which the <a href="https://notdivided.org/"><u>“We Will Not Be Divided” letter</u></a> gathered hundreds of cross-company signatures across Google and OpenAI in support of Anthropic’s red lines, showed that cross-company solidarity is at least possible and perhaps opened a window again for AI safety.</p><p>When your institutions are captured and your market incentives point the wrong direction, the only constraint left is the people who build the thing refusing to build it without conditions.</p><p>But labor leverage only works when talent demand greatly outpaces supply.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjijtxavv0ug"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjijtxavv0ug">[4]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;The implicit threat is always <i>“we could leave”</i> but that only works if leaving creates a problem the lab can’t solve by other means. If AI can do 40% of what a junior safety researcher does today, and that number is climbing double digits annually, the math on <i>“we could leave”</i> changes quickly. And despite what we might want to believe, talent scarcity in safety is potentially a 12-18 month problem, not a 24-36 month one.</p><p>The obvious response to “labor leverage is expiring” is “automate safety research before it expires and the problem solves itself.”</p><p>This seductive version of the future is (mostly) wrong, and the research coming out of the labs themselves explains why we will want humans repeatedly in the loop.</p><h2><strong>Why you can’t automate your way out</strong></h2><p>Safety is conceptually least likely to get automated because the problem is definitionally human(ity) vs. machine.</p><p>Models are getting better at recognizing when they’re being evaluated and modifying their behavior accordingly as <a href="https://openai.com/index/detecting-and-reducing-scheming-in-ai-models/"><u>shown by OpenAI/Apollo noticing o3 underperforming on evals</u></a> to not restrict its deployment, alongside Anthropic’s own <a href="https://assets.anthropic.com/m/983c85a201a962f/original/Alignment-Faking-in-Large-Language-Models-full-paper.pdf"><u>alignment faking research</u></a> demonstrating Claude intentionally pretending to comply with training objectives to avoid modification.</p><p>Capability gains only make this worse. As the model gets more performant, it could get better at being quietly adversarial, and could increase complexity to a point where our dumb human minds can’t comprehend what is or is not happening on any reasonable timescale.</p><p>Evan Hubinger (one of Anthropic’s alignment leads) published a <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/epjuxGnSPof3GnMSL/alignment-remains-a-hard-unsolved-problem"><u>detailed assessment</u></a> in November laying out why the hard parts of alignment haven’t been encountered yet. His argument is that current models are aligned, but only because we’re still in the easy regime where we can directly verify outputs and inspect reasoning. The problems that will actually be difficult (overseeing systems smarter than you, ensuring models generalize correctly on tasks you can’t check, long-horizon RL selecting for power-seeking agents) haven’t meaningfully arrived yet. His first-order solution is to use the latest generation of trustworthy models as automated alignment researchers before we encounter the hard problems, and then to build other models to tackle the hard problems as well.</p><p>Marius Hobbhahn’s <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/bb5Tnjdrptu89rcyY/what-s-the-short-timeline-plan"><i><u>What’s the short timeline plan?</u></i></a> makes a similar point, stating that the minimum viable safety plan requires frontier models to be used overwhelmingly for alignment and security research, with the lab willing to accept months of delay for release for scaled inference.</p><p>These are reasonable plans to a non-AI safety researcher like myself, and yet they are almost certainly unrealistic. Nobody looking to <a href="https://x.com/aakashgupta/status/2026393777401901523"><u>destroy public companies on a near-daily basis</u></a> with product releases and <a href="https://www.reddit.com/r/ClaudeAI/comments/1rbrpmv/software_engineer_position_will_never_die/"><u>obsolete engineers in 12 months</u></a> will want to point their most capable models at the speculative <a href="https://notes.michaeldempsey.me/post/185364608164/moral-obligations-when-investing-in-the-future"><u>Nick Bostrom-style future</u></a> that may or may not come true.</p><p>So my gut tells me on any reasonable timescale, you can’t fully automate your way to proper safety.</p><h2><strong>Where we are</strong></h2><p>Go back to that solidarity letter, because it’s instructive as a high-water mark and could open up an Overton window to establish possible changes.</p><p>The letter represents the best possible conditions for safety-as-labor-leverage by possessing a relatively binary moral issue (mass surveillance, autonomous weapons), a clear antagonist, cross-company support, and Anthropic’s main competitor calling the SCR designation “an extremely scary precedent” while trying to de-escalate.</p><p>The result? Anthropic got designated a supply chain risk. OpenAI got the contract. The red lines are still debatably unclear if they have survived or not as of the last 10 tweets we’ve seen from Sam as he (artfully at times, poorly at others) works through AMAs and not-so-subtweets from multiple employees at his company.</p><p>While the letter brought certain types of “Ideological Safety” to the surface within the AI industry, nobody is going to organize a cross-company petition over eval methodology for deceptive alignment. And you can be damn sure that nobody is building a notdivided.org for compute allocation to interpretability research.</p><p>The realistic safety questions going forward look more like “<i>how much red-teaming is enough before we ship a model that’s better at long-horizon coding tasks and allows us to steamroll Claude Code/Codex usage for 4-8 weeks?</i>”<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefyeqprfosug"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnyeqprfosug">[5]</a></sup></span></p><p>This come-from-behind battle that Anthropic is seemingly winning has coincided with (and one could argue accelerated) the dismantling of the principled commitments that got us here. Anthropic removed the hard-pause provision from its <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/responsible-scaling-policy-v3"><u>Responsible Scaling Policy</u></a> to maintain momentum.&nbsp;<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref867aqx8hq4v"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn867aqx8hq4v">[6]</a></sup></span>OpenAI is not too dissimilar, having a revolving door of talent in their preparedness team alongside the <a href="https://www.cnbc.com/2024/10/24/openai-miles-brundage-agi-readiness.html"><u>dramatic shutting down of their AGI readiness group</u></a> in 2024.<br /><br />And broadly, most binding constraints that underpinned lab safety work are effectively gone, with the latest blog post from Anthropic potentially reading as another form of capitulation as some of the longer-standing talking points of each of these labs were quietly taken out back in a matter of weeks as competition intensified and “faster take off” scenarios emerged.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffif6scjxiar"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfif6scjxiar">[7]</a></sup></span></p><h2><strong>Markets will market</strong></h2><p>Both Anthropic and OpenAI are likely to go public in the next 6-18 months. The two organizations that employ the majority of the world’s frontier safety researchers will answer to public shareholders at exactly the moment when the best plan for safety requires dedicating your most capable model to safety research instead of scaling agents.</p><p>The revenue growth justifying each of the labs’ valuations lives in exactly the use cases where safety review is slowest and most likely to delay a launch<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefong586l7gf"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnong586l7gf">[8]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;and while ideology is great, as we’ve seen, nothing threatens talent moats like <a href="https://mhdempsey.substack.com/i/186576801/vibes-as-competitive-advantage"><u>bad vibes</u></a> or a feeling of under-execution, which will be more clear than ever as each labs’ stock trades second by second.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefwgxwiypo1bl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnwgxwiypo1bl">[9]</a></sup></span></p><p>Once hundreds of thousands of investors are pricing lab equity instead of five, one naturally turns to governments as the next gating mechanism.</p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/rWc6BT8BcoPhwcbSs/ja3cnjqd2tkqbawiyksy" /></figure><p>A <a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/2407.17347"><u>Georgetown paper on AI emergency preparedness</u></a> shows just how unprepared most are, and across the <a href="https://www.mindfoundry.ai/blog/ai-regulations-around-the-world"><u>EU</u></a> (compliance deadlines pushed to 2027-2028), UK<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefqad0hlk1f8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnqad0hlk1f8">[10]</a></sup></span>&nbsp;(voluntary cooperation that labs already ignore), <a href="https://digital.nemko.com/regulations/global-ai-regulations"><u>China</u></a> (state-driven frameworks with different goals entirely), and the US (which revoked its own executive order and used the DPA against the company that held the line), there are very few answers for a <a href="https://internationalaisafetyreport.org/"><u>broad-based consensus</u></a> that AI capabilities are outpacing safety measures and resource constraints.</p><h2><strong>Twelve months…at best</strong></h2><p>Markets always win on a long enough time horizon. Every other constraint on corporate behavior (labor, regulation, institutional commitments, public pressure) either gets priced in, arbitraged away, or restructured into something that serves growth. AI safety is not going to be the exception.</p><p>The question is whether the people who understand the problem can seize this fleeting window of lab skepticism and moral questioning to restructure safety from a moral tax into a commercial moat. Mechanisms that depend on executive goodwill, talent scarcity, or voluntary commitments have a half-life measured in quarters once companies answer to public shareholders.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefbaulthcwv0p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnbaulthcwv0p">[11]</a></sup></span></p><p>To survive the transition to public markets, safety must be priced into the asset. Whatever gets built in the next year has to be embedded in how enterprise deployments are certified, how strict SLAs are met, and how uninsurable liability flows when an autonomous system fails. The kind of thing that persists because it is net-dominant for the business and for the lab to continue to climb up its infinite upside scenario. Again, this has always been the dream “race to the top” scenario.</p><p>Twelve months is not a lot of time to rewire the market structure of an entire industry (and maybe a large portion of societal views). But it is possibly the last time in history the people who care will have any leverage to try.</p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn07jzg692cysl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref07jzg692cysl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>In a similar way that people believed VR was the last device because every other device could live inside of it, AI labs could be the last compounding assets because every other asset can exist within the weights.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnpnb1tqq6b4"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefpnb1tqq6b4">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>I quite enjoyed <a href="https://x.com/finnmurphy12/status/2027140173809754519?s=46&amp;t=42OCfAYVA8zZfQfZtjqP3Q"><u>Finn’s take</u></a> on these labor dynamics.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn93rfqfhkb19"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref93rfqfhkb19">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>At times i wonder if this would be the case if OpenAI didn’t seem to employ the most fucking Online people in all of tech</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjijtxavv0ug"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjijtxavv0ug">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>The main point here really is that AI researchers care about alignment/safety generally, and thus them voting with their feet matters a lot to the perhaps lesser valued AI Safety people</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnyeqprfosug"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefyeqprfosug">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>The one possible structural exception is Google, where antitrust exposure and consumer brand risk accidentally make safety a corporate survival concern. A high-profile safety failure at Google becomes a breakup catalyst in ways that a failure at Anthropic or OpenAI does not. Whether that translates to real alignment investment or better PR is an open question, but it may be the only major AI company where the political and market incentives point in the same direction on safety.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn867aqx8hq4v"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref867aqx8hq4v">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>I would highly recommend reading <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HzKuzrKfaDJvQqmjh/responsible-scaling-policy-v3"><u>Holden’s post contextualizing RSP v3</u></a></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfif6scjxiar"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffif6scjxiar">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>RIP “race to the top”</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnong586l7gf"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefong586l7gf">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>Things like autonomous agents, models in critical infrastructure, AI systems making consequential decisions without human oversight, etc.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnwgxwiypo1bl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefwgxwiypo1bl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>I recognize some of the irony in mentioning talent moats, when I’m also arguing that talent is rapidly losing its power.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnqad0hlk1f8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefqad0hlk1f8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Xwrajm92fdjd7cqnN/what-we-learned-from-briefing-70-lawmakers-on-the-threat"><u>A good post</u></a> on someone who talked to many members of parliament on AI safety.</p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnbaulthcwv0p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefbaulthcwv0p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p>To be clear, that might just mean create a social construct that then works into actually built systems or regulation, or it might mean gunning for more compute resource commitments that set industry standards. There are many half-baked ideas.</p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/rWc6BT8BcoPhwcbSs/ai-safety-has-12-months-left#comments">Discuss</a>
